Reviewer #2 (Remarks to the Author):
In this manuscript, the authors reported a conductive MOF of Ni-BTA for highly-efficient
electrocatalytic H2O2 production. According to theoretical calculation, d-OH-Ni-BTA is expected to
have the best performance among all samples. Then plentiful characterizations and tests were carried
out to verify the theoretical prediction. The catalyst activity and stability for H2O2 production in
neutral and alkaline conditions were investigated in detail, and the sample was also applied for
practical applications of dye degradation and disinfection. It is a topic of interest to the researchers in
the related areas, but there are still some major issues need to be addressed before recommendation
for publication. The detailed comments are as follows:
1. In Figure 2d, the author indicated that all the M-BTA are crystalline in the main text, but based on
the results, only Ni-TBA has obvious peaks, for other materials, they tend to be amorphous. In
Supplementary Fig. 12 and Supplementary Fig. 15, it was found that amorphous sample had worse
performance than crystalline sample, so, actually, it is not accurate to compare the performance of
Ni-TBA with other M-BTAs. The authors should get higher crystalline samples of Fe/Co/Cu/Mn-
TBA and then do the comparation. Besides, it is better to add simulated results in the XRD spectra
for comparation.
Response: Firstly, we would like to thank the reviewer for his/her very positive comments and
valuable suggestions, which will definitely help us further improve the quality of this work.
Indeed, the amorphous sample has inferior performance to the crystalline sample, according to
Supplementary Figs. 12 and 15 (Supplementary Figs. 16 and 24 in the revised manuscript).
However, the amorphous Ni-BTA still delivers a higher 2e--ORR performance (i.e., H2O2 selectivity
over 80% in a wide voltage range for a-Ni-BTA) than those of the other amorphous M-BTAs (Fig.
3b and Supplementary 24b). This result could give a preliminary conclusion of the higher 2e--ORR
performance for Ni-BTA than the other M-BTAs.
To more accurately compare the performance of Ni-TBA with other M-BTAs, we have tried our
best to prepare higher crystalline samples of Fe/Co/Cu/Mn-TBA. Among them, the crystallinity of
Cu-BTA could be obviously improved by lowering the nucleation rate and prolonging the growth
time (Supplementary Fig. 18). According to the LSV test (Supplementary Fig. 25), the high-
crystallinity Cu-BTA (H-Cu-BTA) shows an enhanced 2e--ORR performance (H2O2 selectivity nearly
10
80%) in comparison with the previous amorphous Cu-BTA. However, it is still lower than that of Ni-
BTA sample (high crystallinity). This result is consistent with the DFT calculation results, where the
d-OH-Cu shows a too-high ΔG*OOH value of ~4.56 eV compared to that of d-OH-Ni (4.21 eV), and
confirms the better 2e--ORR performance of Ni-BTA than Cu-BTA. And the improved 2e--ORR
performance of H-Cu-BTA compared with the Cu-BTA should be attributed to the enhanced ratio of
ordered layer-structure in the sample (meanwhile the d-/d-OH-Cu possess a closer ΔG*OOH to 4.23 V
than that of s-/s-OH-Cu). A similar conclusion could also be derived from the enhanced 2e--ORR
performance of Ni-BTA (high-crystallinity) compared with the amorphous counterpart. Therefore,
the two typical examples provide additional evidence to confirm the positive effect of H-bond
(originating from the layered structure of the materials) enhanced 2e--ORR activity and selectivity
for the low-activity catalytic sites (i.e., s-Ni/s-Cu).
Unfortunately, we were not able to successfully prepare high-crystallinity Fe/Co/Mn-TBA
samples, although a number of methods have been tried, including the common growth kinetics
regulations (lowing the reaction temperature, reducing the concentration of reactants) and sequential
ligand exchange strategy, etc. The detailed methods for synthesizing Co-BTA, as an example, and the
corresponding XRD results are provided in Fig. R1. Despite this, it should still be sufficiently
reasonable to believe that the Ni-BTA delivers a much better 2e--ORR performance than those of
Fe/Co/Mn-TBA. The difference between high-crystallinity and amorphous structure for the BTA-
based MOFs is the ratio of ordered layered structure. Meanwhile, in our study, the H-bond formation
via the dual-layer structure is proposed to enhance the adsorption capacity to *OOH intermediates,
but the Co/Fe/Mn-BTA with dual-layer structure all show too strong adsorption capacity to *OOH
intermediates (<4.23 eV), ~4.05 eV for d-OH-Co, 3.95 eV for d-OH-Fe and 3.61 eV for d-OH-Mn in
theory. That is, the high-crystallinity Fe/Co/Mn-BTA should deliver a lower 2e--ORR performance
than the counterpart with an amorphous structure. Thus, we can conclude that the Ni-BTA (high-
crystallinity), which possesses better 2e--ORR performance than the amorphous Fe/Co/Mn-BTA, is
bound to have even better 2e--ORR catalytic performance than those Fe/Co/Mn-BTA with high-
crystallinity.
To sum up, based on the discussion and the experimental result, it is reasonable to believe that
Ni-BTA possesses better performance than the other M-BTA samples. To better illustrate this and to
11
avoid confusing the readers, we have made the following revision to the manuscript:
On Page 9, we have added “… In addition, to further confirm the H-bond enhanced 2e--ORR
activity for the catalytic sites with weak OOH* adsorption capacity (i.e., s-Cu and s-Ni), the Cu-BTA
with higher crystallinity (ordered layered structure), compared to that of Cu-BTA prepared with the
same method of Ni-BTA, was also prepared by lowering the nucleation rate and prolonging the
growth time (Supplementary Figs. 18). The district XRD peaks of H-Cu-BTA, with similar positions
as the Ni-BTA, as well its rob-like morphology, collaboratively confirm its largely enhanced ordered
structure. Specifically, the effect of crystallinity for the materials on the 2e--ORR performance will
be discussed in the following. …”.
Correspondingly, the characterization and catalytic performance of high-crystallinity Cu-BTA
(H-Cu-BTA) are provided in Supplementary Fig. 18, 25 as follows in the revised manuscript.
And on Page 12, we have added: “…Similarly, the high-crystallinity Cu-BTA (H-Cu-BTA) also
delivers an enhanced 2e--ORR performance (H2O2 selectivity nearly 80%) compared with the
amorphous Cu-BTA, but this performance is still lower than that of Ni-BTA sample with high-
crystallinity (Supplementary Fig. 25). Although the high-crystallinity Mn/Fe/Co-BTAs were not
successfully prepared, it would be rational to believe that the Ni-BTA (high-crystallinity) would
deliver better 2e--ORR performance than the Fe/Co/Mn-BTA (either amorphous or high-crystallinity),
and the reasons are as follows: in our study, the H-bond formation via the dual-layer structure is
proposed to enhance the adsorption capacity to *OOH intermediates, and the Co/Fe/Mn-BTA with
dual-layer structure all show too strong adsorption capacity to *OOH intermediates (<4.23 eV), ~4.05
eV for d-OH-Co, 3.95 eV for d-OH-Fe and 3.61 eV for d-OH-Mn in theory. That is, the high-
crystallinity Fe/Co/Mn-BTA should deliver a lower 2e--ORR performance than the counterpart with
an amorphous structure. Thus, we conclude that the Ni-BTA possess the intrinsic better 2e--ORR
performance than those Mn/Fe/Co/Mn-BTAs. …”

Note: the details on preparing H-Cu-BTA include diluting the concentration of the reactants (by
increasing the volume of deionized water from the previous 20 mL in total to 500 mL in total),
decreasing the amount of NH3·H2O (decreasing the volume of concentrated aqueous ammonia (~14
M) from 1.0 mL to 0.1 mL), and prolonging the reaction time to 2 days from previous 6 h.
Supplementary Fig. 25. The LSV curves (a) and the calculated H2O2 selectivity (b) of H-Cu-BTA

14
Fig. R1. The XRD patterns of Co-BTA prepared via different methods.
Note: Low themperature means stirring the reactants in 4 ℃ for 24 h;
Diluted solution means diluting the concentration of the reactants (by increasing the volume of
deionized water from the previous 20 mL in total to 500 mL in total), decreasing the amount of
NH3·H2O (decreasing the volume of concentrated aqueous ammonia (~14 M) from 1.0 mL to 0.1
mL), and prolonging the reaction time to 2 days from previous 6 h.
Selecting the surface product means only collecting the surficial product. Owing to the gas-
liquid interface, the product in the interface commonly shows a metallic lustre, which may possess a
high crystallinity different from the internal composition.
Adding NMP means the addition of 10% of 1-methyl-2-pyrrolidone (NMP) to the volume of
deionized water during the reaction. And the NMP would contribute to a larger crystal for the Co-
based c-MOFs, according to the previous works (Chem. Mater. 2012, 24, 3511-3513).
Adding PAN means adding 3.0 mmol phenylamine molecules (PAN, 10 equiv to BTA), which
the -NH2 group in PAN would first form a coordination structure, and BTA would exchange with
PAN. This method is named the “sequential ligand exchange strategy” and has already been adopted
to prepare high-crystallinity, even single-crystal samples (Science 2018, 361, 48–52, Angew. Chem.
Int. Ed. 2023, e202313591).
Adding PAN at 60 ℃ means increasing the reaction temperature to study the effect of the
increased temperature on the crystallinity of the samples.

2. The theoretical work is very impressive and in detail, however, the key challenge is how to get the
predicted structure. The authors did many characterizations to confirm the structure of d-OH-Ni-BTA,
but I am still concern whether using the sample of d-Ni-BTA can get similar results. First, Ni-BTA is
a typical conductive MOF, and the synthetic method in this work is conventional and similar to
reported ones (for example, Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 2019, 58,14731 –14739), but without specifical
modification in the synthesis procedure, how to ensure the sample of d-OH-Ni-BTA instead of d-Ni-
BTA? Besides, in Supplementary Fig. 5, it seems that there are no signal of OH group in the IR spectra.
And it is difficult to get convincing evidence from the XPS results since O signals can be obtained
from many resources, which are not easy to be distinguished. Moreover, in Fig. 2g, could you also
provide the result of fitting curve for d-Ni-TBA to further confirm the structure?
Response: We would like to thank the reviewer for this very insightful comment. Firstly, in the
previous works (Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 2019, 58,14731-14739), although the authors did not clearly
point out the existence of an axial-O in the metal centers in Ni-BTA, they indeed confirmed the
existence of absorbed O-containing species in Ni-BTA such as H2O and solvents in Figure S9 and
Table S1 of this paper. Meanwhile, according to the previous works on c-MOFs (Science 2022, 377,
335-339, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2022, 144, 15845-15854), the absorbed H2O or other O-containing
species would play a major role in the formation of axial-O configurations (e.g., Ni-OH) at the metal
centers.
Secondly, in the IR spectra in our study, the signal of the -OH group commonly appears in the
range of 3000-3500 cm-1, which almost overlaps with the position of -NH- groups. In the IR spectra
17
for all M-BTA samples in our study, the peaks at ~3300 cm-1 are broad (Supplementary Fig. 8),
indicating the possibility of the emergence of -OH groups but also bringing difficulty to ensure
whether -OH exists. To clarify this, we further carried out the elemental analysis (EA) on the Ni-BTA
to confirm the content of C, H, O, and N elements by Vario Micro Cube Elemental Analyzer and the
content of Ni was measured by ICP. According to the normalized experimental results, the atomic
ratio of C, N, O, H, and Ni is about 7.1: 4.4 :1.4 :8.0 :1.0 (i.e., Ni1.0 C7.1N4.4O1.4H8.0), close to the
theoretical results (i.e., Ni1.0C6.0N4.0O1.4H6.0), but with extra O-atoms (~1.4:1 to Ni-atoms) in the
experiment. This experiment further provides convincing evidence to ensure the sample of d-OH-Ni-
BTA instead of d-Ni-BTA.
Thirdly, according to the reviewer’s suggestion, we have re-fixed the XAFS spectra of Ni-BTA
with three different configurations, including Ni-N4 representing s-Ni-BTA or d-Ni-BTA and Ni-
N4O/Ni-N4O1.5 representing d-OH-Ni-BTA (Fig. 2g and Supplementary Figs. 12-13). As shown in
the following Supplementary Table 3, the R factor for fitting the Ni-BTA with Ni-N4 (10.5%) is
much higher than 2%, which indicates an unreasonable fitting result, and the Ni centers are thus not
merely coordinated by four N atoms in the prepared d-OH-Ni-BTA sample. When adding the axial-
O species, the R factor rapidly decreased. Especially, the Ni-N4O1.5 configuration shows the most
reasonable fitting result according to its smallest R factor of 1.0%, thus confirming the existence of
axial-O structure in the prepared d-OH-Ni-BTA sample and providing clear evidence to ensure the
prepared sample is d-OH-Ni-BTA rather than d-Ni-BTA. Of note, the Ni-N4O1.5 configuration (i.e.,
Ni center coordinated by 4 N and 1.5 O in average) was proposed according to the element analysis
and ICP results, as mentioned above (Supplementary Table 2).

Table 2), which provides reliable rationality for the proposed M-BTA models with the out-of-plane -

On Page 9, we made a revision in the revised manuscript: “… The quantitative least-squares
EXAFS fitting analysis further confirms the existence of extra axial-O (using -OH as the example of
axial-O groups) on the Ni sites apart from the Ni-(NH)4 coordination configuration (Fig. 2g and
Supplementary Figs. 12, 13), according to the more reasonable fitting results of Ni-N4O/Ni-N4O1.5
configurations than that of Ni-N4 configuration in theory (Supplementary Table 3). Of note, the Ni-
21
N4O1.5 configuration (the Ni-center is coordinated by average 4 N and 1.5 O atoms) was proposed
according to the element analysis and ICP results (Supplementary Table 2). On the basis of these
considerations, it is reasonable to conclude that the d-OH-Ni-BTA model is more reasonable than the
d-Ni-BTA or s-Ni-BTA configurations to represent the actual coordination environment of Ni centers
in the as-prepared Ni-BTA sample. …”
3. H2O2 is an important chemical and it is more stable and promising in acid condition, the H2O2
selectivity of the best sample in the work in alkaline condition is average given that many reported
materials can achieve a high selectivity over 90% (Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 2022, 61, e202117347;
Energy Environ. Sci., 2022, 15, 4167–4174), but in neutral electrolyte, the performance is good. And
it will be better to try acid condition as well.
Response: Thanks for this very valuable suggestion. According to this, the 2e--ORR performance of
Ni-BTA in acid conditions has also been tested in O2-saturated 0.1 M HClO4 solution using RRDE at
1600 rpm. As shown in Fig. R2, although the selectivity of Ni-BTA in acidic medium is around 80%
in a range of 0-0.5 V vs. RHE, the ring current is fairly small (0.005-0.01 mA), only equivalent to 5%
of that achieved in the neutral electrolyte (~0.16 mA). Such low H2O2 production current density
indicates the poor 2e--ORR performance of Ni-BTA in acidic electrolytes.
In addition, with the increase of scanning cycles, both the disk and the ring current densities
decreased rapidly (there was no interval between different scanning times), which indicates that the
catalytic performance of Ni-BTA in acidic electrolytes was unstable. This result may be induced by
the poor stability of Ni-BTA in the acid electrolyte, which is evidenced by the color change from
transparent before the test (30 mins stirring and 30 mins rest) to red after the test in 0.1 M HClO4.
This is different from the transparent electrolyte solution before and after the test in 0.1 M KOH and
3.6 wt.% NaCl solutions (Fig. R3), further confirming the unsatisfactory stability of Ni-BTA in acidic
conditions.
To sum up, the Ni-BTA is not suitable as a 2e--ORR catalyst in acidic conditions, and to avoid
misleading the readers, the 2e--ORR testing results in acidic electrolytes are not included in the
revised manuscript.

HClO4 solution at 10 mV s-1, (b) the calculated H2O2 selectivity.
Fig. R3. The photos of Ni-BTA in 0.1 M KOH, 3.6 wt.% NaCl and 0.1 M HClO4 solutions after 30
mins stirring and rest for 30 mins.
4. The turnover frequency (TOF) is an important parameter to evaluate the intrinsic activity of
catalysts, it would be better to calculate the TOFs of the samples.
Response: Thanks for this comment. According to the previous works (i.e., Nat. Catal. 2023, 6, 234-
243), the TOFs of the Ni-BTA in 1 M KOH and 1 M NaCl electrolytes are calculated based on its 2e-
23
-ORR catalytic performance in flow cells on the basis of the LSV results in Fig. 4b, which could
better illustrate the intrinsic activity of catalysts under practical operation conditions.
During the calculations, it was assumed that all Ni centers were actively involved in 2e--ORR
and that the content of Ni in the material was set at 24.5 wt.% according to the ICP results. The TOFs
reached 1.56 s−1 and 2.27 s−1 in 1 M NaCl and 1 M KOH electrolytes when the potentials were -0.59
V vs. RHE and -0.42 V vs. RHE, respectively (Supplementary Fig. 28). And this result is comparable
to most of the state-of-the-art MOFs- and compound-based catalysts (Nat. Commun. 2022, 13, 2721,
Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 2023, 62, e202307355).
To better illustrate this point, the calculated TOFs are added as Supplementary Fig. 28, and the
details of TOF calculation are given in the Supporting note in the Supporting Information as follows.
Supplementary Fig. 28. TOFs of Ni-BTA catalyst derived from three-compartment flow cell results
in 1M KOH and 1M NaCl solutions, respectively.
Note: The turnover frequency (TOF) was calculated based on the following equation:
𝑇𝑂𝐹=
𝐼𝐻2𝑂2 ∗𝑀𝑁𝑖
𝑛𝐹∗𝑀𝑐𝑎𝑡∗𝜔𝑁𝑖
where 𝐼𝐻2𝑂2 is the current for H2O2 production; 𝑀𝑁𝑖 is the molar mass of cobalt (58.69 g mol-1); n is
the number of transferred electrons; F is the Faraday constant (96485 C mol-1); 𝑀𝑐𝑎𝑡 is the mass of
catalyst on the electrode; 𝜔𝑁𝑖 is the mass ratio of Ni in the catalyst.
On Page 13, we have added: “… Additionally, it was assumed that all Ni centers were actively
24
involved in 2e--ORR and that the content of Ni in the material was set at 24.5 wt.% according to the
ICP results. The TOFs reached 1.56 s−1 and 2.27 s−1 in 1 M NaCl and 1 M KOH electrolytes, when
the potentials were at -0.59 V vs. RHE and -0.42 V vs. RHE, respectively (Supplementary Fig. 28),
indicating the high intrinsic activity of the catalyst. …”

6. The real pH value of the electrolytes of 0.1 M KOH, 0.9 wt.% NaCl, 3.6 wt.% NaCl, and 0.1 M
Na2SO4 should be measured and noted.
Response: The pH values of the electrolytes of 0.1 M KOH, 0.9 wt.% NaCl, 3.6 wt.% NaCl, and 0.1
M Na2SO4 are measured to ~13.0, ~7.2, ~6.7, and ~7.0, respectively, in our study, and we are sorry
that the pH values of the electrolytes were not provided in the original manuscript. The pH values of
1.0 M KOH and 1M NaCl are ~13.8 and ~6.5 in our study, and we have also noted this in the revised
manuscript.
To better note this, on Page 19, we have added “…The LSV curves were measured on the RRDE
at a scan rate of 10 mV s-1 and a rotating speed of 1600 rpm in O2-saturated alkaline and neutral
medium, including 0.1 M KOH (pH ~13.0), 0.9 wt. % NaCl (pH ~7.2), 3.6 wt. % NaCl (pH ~6.7) and

Reviewer #3 (Remarks to the Author):
The authors present the ORR performance of Ni-BTA, a typical 1D c-MOF with a layered structure
and abundant Ni-(NH)4 moieties. Their as-prepared catalyst exhibits an electrocatalytic 2e--ORR
performances under neutral and alkaline conditions (e.g., >85% H2O2 selectivity from -0.1-0.4 V vs.
RHE and a >13.5 mol g-1 h-1 H2O2 yield in neutral electrolytes). A computational investigation on
possible active sites of Ni-BTA is presented to show the preference for the 2e- ORR pathway over the
4e- ORR pathway. An MD-based computational simulation to determine kinetic barriers from the
*OOH species to the product valley is compared to potential dependent in-situ ATR-SEIRAS data
and discussed. A clear dependence of the electrolyte is observed on the Faradaic efficiency and yield,
with 1M KOH identify as best performing electrolyte, resulting in the highest FE and yield for H2O2.
Finally, to show the applicability of the synthesis catalyst, its antibacterial properties are studied on
E. Coli and S. aureus, using the 0.9 wt.% electrolyte.
In my opinion, the work performed is interesting to for researchers in the field of electrochemistry
and material science. It is sometimes hard or impossible to find at which conditions experimental data
has been gathered because information is not supplied in SI, nor in main manuscript, which makes
one to scroll back and forth in the manuscript and SI without finding the conditions. Furthermore,
gaps in knowledge and limitations of the applied techniques are not addressed nor discussed. Before
this study can be considered for publication in Nature Communications, the following items need to
be addressed;
1. Comparison of the performance with other ORR catalyst from literature, comparing different
performance parameters (selectivity, operating range, yield, electrolyte).

2. How is this MOF-type electrocatalyst anchored to the electrode? Can the charge transfer
limitations be quantified?
Response: In our study, the catalysts are coated onto the gas-diffusion-electrode (GDE) in the
presence of PTFE binder. The contact between the catalysts and the electrode is sufficient, owing to
the large specific surface aera of the porous GDE, the nano-scale size of Ni-BTA catalysts, and the
assistance of the PTFE binder. Thus, it is reasonable to believe that the charge transfer between the
catalyst and electrode is relatively facile.
According to the reviewer’s suggestion, EIS test at the open-circuit potential in 1 M KOH and 1
M NaCl electrolytes was carried out to quantify the charge transfer between the MOF-type
electrocatalyst and the GDE electrode in the flow cell. First, the ultrahigh-frequency intersection at
the real axes the Nyquist plots is assigned to the solution resistance, and the semicircular trace in the
high-frequency region reflects the charge transfer resistance (Rct) between the MOF-type
electrocatalyst and the GDE electrode in the flow cell. The fitting results (referred to Nat. Commun.
2020, 11: 4233 and Nano Res. 2022, 15 (1): 304-309) confirm a low Rct between the MOF-type
electrocatalyst and the electrode, which is as small as ~0.85 Ω and ~0.70 Ω in 1 M NaCl and 1 M
KOH electrolytes respectively. The small Rct would be attributed to the relatively good electron
conductivity of the Ni-BTA MOFs and the close contact between the catalyst and the gas-diffusion
electrode.
Additionally, in the fitting result, the Rs is assigned to the solution resistance, CF represents the
double-layer capacitance of the catalyst layer (CDL), and Warburg impedance (Zw) takes into account
a finite diffusion. The relatively large Rs (~4.53 Ω in 1 M KOH and 4.92 Ω in 1 M NaCl) may be
induced by the large distance (~2 cm) between the anode and cathode in our flow cell, which increases
the voltage loss of the solutions. Thus, an efficient strategy to reduce the full-cell voltage and energy
consumption of H2O2 electrosynthesis is to reduce the distance between the cathode and anode, which
30
contributes to a small Rs.
Supplementary Fig. 26. Electrochemical impedance spectroscopy (EIS) curves and the fitting results
of Ni-BTA catalyst in 1M KOH and 1M NaCl solutions at the open-circuit potential in the gas-
diffusion electrode-based flow-cell, and the EIS was carried out under an alternating current
perturbation voltage of 5 mV in the frequency range of 105 Hz to 0.1 Hz.
To better illustrate this, on Page 13 we added: “… Before the H2O2 accumulation test,
electrochemical impedance spectroscopy (EIS) test confirms a low charge transfer resistance (~0.85
Ω and ~0.70 Ω in 1M NaCl and 1M KOH solutions respectively) between the MOF-type
electrocatalyst and the electrode (Supplementary Fig. 26), which would be attributed to the relatively
good electron conductivity of the Ni-BTA MOFs and the close contact between the catalyst and the
porous gas-diffusion electrode29-31. …”
And the EIS curves and the fitting result are provided as Supplementary Fig. 26, as above.
Meanwhile the corresponidng references are cited in the revised as follows:
... 29. Dong, K. et al. Conductive Two-dimensional magnesium metal-organic frameworks for high-
efficiency O2 electroreduction to H2O2. ACS Catal. 12, 6092-6099 (2022).
30. Lien, H.-T. et al. Probing the active site in single-atom oxygen reduction catalysts via operando
X-ray and electrochemical spectroscopy. Nat. Commun. 11, 4233 (2020).
31. Zhang, L. et al. N-doped carbon nanotubes supported CoSe2 nanoparticles: A highly efficient and
stable catalyst for H2O2 electrosynthesis in acidic media. Nano Res. 15, 304-309 (2022). …
3. The emergence of various types of *OD species in the ATR-SEIRAS spectra is not discussed in the
manuscript? Can the authors rationalize this via computational spectroscopy?
31
Response: We would like to thank the reviewer for this insightful comment. According to the
reviewer’s suggestion and to better understand the ATR-SEIRAS results, the theoretical characteristic
vibration frequency of the infrared absorption spectrum of different oxygen-containing species in
D2O solution was firstly calculated with the exchange-correlation functional b3lyp in Gaussian 09.
Deuterium atoms in the intermediates were set in the input files. The basis sets for atoms of
investigated molecules were 6-31+G(d).
The models of different oxygen-containing species absorbed on Ni-center in Ni-BTA are shown
in Fig. R4, and the calculated results are shown in Supplementary Table 7. There is a red-shift of
~180 cm-1 for all peaks (i.e., 1306.22 cm-1 vs. 1480 cm-1 for O2ab, and 1031.48 cm-1 vs. 1210 cm-1 for
*OOD), which may be induced by the effect of test conditions or solvation (Phys. Chem. Chem. Phys.
2013, 15, 5771−5781). Despite this, it is still rational and feasible to predict the vibration frequency
of the infrared absorption spectrum via this method. Correspondingly, the Ni-OD stretching vibration
peak should be located at ~2900 cm-1 (with a red-shift compared to that calculated 2740.97 cm-1), but
there is no obvious absorption peak in this region in the ATR-SEIRAS spectra (Fig. 5a). This result
indicates the favorable 2e--ORR pathway rather than 4e--pathway for the Ni center in our catalyst. It
also confirms that the exposed Ni sites will not be covered by OD species under ORR conditions, in
agreement with the result concluded from the surface Pourbaix diagrams in comment 6.
To better illustrate this, on page 15, we added: “… according to the previous works32 and the
calculated results via computational spectroscopy (Supplementary Table 7). And there is no obvious
signal assigned to Ni-OD stretching vibration absorption peak (around 2900 cm-1), indicating the
favorable 2e--ORR pathway rather than 4e--ORR pathway for the Ni center in our catalyst. It also
confirms that the exposed Ni sites will not be covered by OD species under ORR condition, in
agreement with the result concluded from the surface Pourbaix diagrams (Supplementary Fig. 40)
33, 34. …”
Correspondingly, the methods and results of computational spectroscopy are added in
Supplementary Table 7 and the following notes in the revised manuscript, as follows.

in 3.6 wt.% NaCl solution, and the ex-situ XRD was also performed after 7-hour catalysis process. In
addition, the ex-situ TEM images are collected after 2-hour and 7-hour catalytic process, respectively.
As shown in Supplementary Fig. 32, the three characteristic peaks of Ni-BTA were well
preserved during the catalysis and after 7 h of catalysis, and no other peaks were observed besides a
weak one at ~26.4°, from the carbon-fiber matrix, suggesting the excellent stability of the as-prepared
catalyst during catalytic process. In addition, the well-retained rob-like morphology of Ni-BTA after
2 h and 7 h of catalysis further confirms the structural stability of the electrocatalyst (Supplementary
Fig. 33).

5. There was no discussion on the purity of the applied electrolyte solutions, and how this might affect
the results.
Response: We agree that the purity of the applied electrolyte solutions may affect the material’s
catalytic performance, especially the metal impurities (e.g., Fe3+ and Ca2+ ions etc.). During the ORR
process, the formed alkalescent environment on the surface of the catalysts (during the transformation
of adsorbed O2 to OOH and HOOH) would lead to the deposition of metal ions (e.g., Fe3+ to Fe(OH)x)
onto the electrode surface and then affect the intrinsic activity of the catalysts. Secondly, under
neutral/acid conditions, the enriched metal ions around the catalytic sites may decrease the hydrogen
evolution activity and then widen the ORR potential range of catalysts (Nat. Commun. 2022, 12,
2880). That is, the influence of electrolyte purity on the performance of catalysts is fairly complex.
Because we mainly focus on the possible hydrogen bond effect on materials’ ORR catalytic capability
in this work, the effect of electrolyte solution purity was not investigated in-depth, and all the
chemicals and solvents were used as received. We will definitely conduct research in this field in the
future, according to the reviewer’s suggestion.
In order to more clearly show our testing conditions, the electrolyte purity, manufacturer, and
other relevant information are provided in the section of “Methods” on Page 18 in the revised
manuscript: “… All chemicals were directly used without further purification, … KOH (AR, 95.0%)
and Na2SO4 (AR, 99%) were purchased from Aladdin Reagents. NaCl (GR, 99.8%) was purchased
from Macklin. …”
6. More in dept modelling or computational studies, towards identifying the active sites are most
dominant under ORR conditions; for example, via surface Pourbaix diagrams, and decomposition
36
studies for varies M-BTA systems to compare the active site model systems s-Ni, d-Ni, s-OH-Ni, and
d-OH-Ni.
Response: We would like to thank the reviewer for this insightful comment. As suggested, we have
performed more in-depth computational studies, including the surface Pourbaix diagrams and
decomposition calculations for various M-BTA systems, as follows.
The structure of the catalyst surface may be affected by the applied potential. Therefore, we
firstly investigated the electrochemical stability of different configurations in Ni-BTA and the most
stable reactive surface at 0.7 V by constructing surface Pourbaix diagrams (J. Mater. Chem. A, 2021,
9, 26019-26027. Nat. Commun. 2019, 10, 3997.). As shown in Supplementary Fig. 40, s-Ni, s-OH-
Ni, d-Ni, and d-OH-Ni are all stable with pristine surface structure over a wide range of potential due
to their low oxygen affinity. It is worth noting that pH can also impact the catalyst structure (ACS
Catal. 2023, 14, 45-55.), and thus, we introduced the role of pH in the Pourbaix diagrams. The E-pH
plots deliver a similar conclusion (Supplementary Fig. 40b) that all configurations in Ni-BTA are
stable under the working conditions, and the exposed metal Ni sites will not be covered by *O or
*OH under ORR conductions. Thus, we could conclude that the exposed Ni sites will maintain their
original structure (d-OH-Ni) and stably catalytic O2 towards H2O2.
In addition, supplementary calculations were also performed for the four M-BTA (M=Mn, Fe,
Co and Cu) systems with different configurations (s-M, OH-M, d-M and d-OH-M). As shown in
Supplementary Fig. 2, the variation of ΔG*OOH of different metal species in the four configurations
of s, s-OH, d and d-OH is consistent, which are all in the order of Mn-BTA<Fe-BTA<Co-BTA<Ni-
BTA<Cu-BTA. Although s-OH-Co (single-layer Co-BTA) is also located near the top of the volcano
plot, it could not be successfully synthesized experimentally. Thus, for the Co-BTA particles in our
study, the d-OH-Co configuration should be the reasonable configuration to represent the
coordination environment of the active metal centers.
37

Ni and d-OH-Ni.
Supplementary Fig. 2. Calculated catalytic activity volcano plots for 2e- and 4e--ORR of M-BTA.
To better express this point, we have modified the manuscript as follows:

Correspondingly, the related references are cited as follows:

7. Insights in the error bar on the kinetic barriers (from MD simulations) are required, since
variations in the water structure will lead to other barriers.
Response: Indeed, we agree with the reviewer’s opinion that variations in water structure can lead to
other barriers. In the available reports on thermodynamic calculations, the effect of the solution is
usually introduced by using an implicit solution model (Nat. Commun. 2023, 14, 4430.) or adding a
solvation correction (ACS Catal. 2019, 9. 11042-11054.). In the kinetic calculations, due to technical
limitations, we are temporarily unable to accurately account for the barriers induced by changes in
water structure during the MD simulation. Therefore, to minimize the error caused by the variation
of the solution network on the potential barrier, we have performed three different types of slow-
growth kinetic simulations for the 2e- and 4e--ORR of d-OH-Ni and provided the kinetic energy
barriers with error bars. As shown in Supplementary Fig. 44, the average energy barriers of the ORR
through the 2e- ORR path (0.30 eV) by breaking the C-O bond are significantly lower than those (1.22
eV) of the 4e- ORR path by breaking the O-O bond, further confirming that d-OH-Ni would follow
the energetically more favorable 2e--ORR pathway, resulting in the rapid and selective formation of
H2O2. And this conclusion will not be affected by the barriers of water variation.
39
Supplementary Fig. 44. Kinetic energy barriers (with error bars) for 2e- and 4e--ORR from slow-
growth kinetic simulations.
To better express this point, on page 17, we have added: “… In addition, to reduce the effect of
solution network variation on the barrier, we performed several slow-growth kinetic simulations
(Supplementary Fig. 44), and the similar results obtained from these simulations (i.e., much higher
energy required to dissociate the strong O-O bond than that of the Ni-O bond) confirm that d-OH-Ni
would follow the energetically more favorable 2e--ORR pathway, resulting in the rapid and selective
formation of H2O2. …”
8. XRD and EXAFS pre and post-electrocatalysis.
Response: Thanks for this suggestion. Accordingly, the XRD patterns pre- and post-electrocatalysis
were performed, as shown in response to Comment 4#, and the post-electrocatalysis EXAFS was
also collected after 7 h catalysis, as shown in Supplementary Fig. 34. In comparison to the initial
XAFS results of Ni-BTA, there is no obvious change in the spectra obtained after the catalysis,
including the Ni K-edge EXAFS spectra and the k2-weighted EXAFS spectra at R-space
(Supplementary Fig. 34 a, b), indicating a relatively stable chemical and coordination structure for
Ni-BTA during catalysis. Correspondingly, the EXAFS fitting result also confirms that the Ni-N4Ox
coordination configuration has been retained after the catalysis test (Supplementary Fig. 34 c, d). In
addition, there is a small shift of the Ni K-edge XANES spectrum to higher energy after the catalysis,
referring to an increased oxidation state for the central Ni atom, which may arise from the absorbed
oxygen-containing species during the ORR process that are not totally desorbed. Thus, a smaller R-
40
factor of 0.6% has been obtained by adopting the Ni-N4O2 configuration than that of the Ni-N4O1.5
configuration (1.4 %), further confirming the extra absorbed axial-O species after the ORR catalysis
(Supplementary Fig. 34 e, f, and Supplementary Table 6).

RESPONSE TO REVIEWER COMMENTS
Reviewer #1 (Remarks to the Author):
All my concerns have been well addressed and the current revision is recommended for acceptance.
Reviewer #2 (Remarks to the Author):

Reviewer #3 (Remarks to the Author):
The manuscript has improved a lot and the authors have made efforts to improve the manuscript based
on all reviewers' comments. However, there are still some remaining items that need further revisions;
a selection of the items which need to be addressed.
1. The methodology used for EXAFS fitting, and tools used are not discussed.
Response: Firstly, we would like to thank the reviewer for his/her very positive comments and
valuable suggestions, which will definitely help us further improve the quality of this work.
The EXAFS raw data were background-subtracted, normalized, and Fourier transformed by
standard procedures using ATHENA module implemented in the IFEFFIT software package. Least-
squares curve fitting analyses of the EXAFS χ(k) data were carried out using the ARTEMIS program
in the desired k-range and R-range with k3-weighted. The above software is downloaded at
https://bruceravel.github.io/demeter/, and the detailed methods for pre-processing and fitting EXAFS
2
data can also be found on this website.
During the process of EXAFS fitting, S02 (amplitude reduction factor) was first fixed according
to the experimental EXAFS fit of NiPc by fixing coordination number (CN) as the known
crystallographic value, which was further verified in NiO and Ni foil. Then a fixed Ni-N coordination
number of four was pre-set in the first coordination sphere of Ni-BTA, meanwhile tailoring the axial-
O structure (without or with a different number of Ni-O path), in which the Ni-N path is according to
the Ni-N bonds in NiPc and the Ni-O path is according to the Ni-O bonds in NiO. The ΔR (the
difference of the bond length between the experimental value and the reference path) for each path
was allowed to vary independently, while ΔE0 and σ2 were allowed to vary dependently when fitting
the Ni-BTA EXAFS. A reasonable range of EXAFS fitting parameters is: CN > 0; σ2 > 0 Å2; ΔE0 <
10 eV; R factor < 0.02 (2.0%). Note: R is the distance between absorber and backscatter atoms; σ2 is
the Debye-Waller factor to account for both thermal and structural disorders; ΔE0 is inner potential
correction; and R-factor indicates the goodness of the fit, a smaller value indicates a better fitting
result.
To better illustrate this, we make a revision on Page 19: “…Specifically, the Athena module of
the IFEFFIT software package was utilized to analyze the XAFS raw data according to standard
procedures, and the EXAFS data fitting was performed on the Artemis program…”
In addition, the details of EXAFS fitting are added in the Supporting Information following
Supplementary Table 3, as follows: “…Note: The Ni-N path is according to the Ni-N bonds in NiPc,
and the Ni-O path is according to the Ni-O bonds in NiO. During fitting the NiPc standard sample,
the three paths are all from the NiPc model, including the single scattering path of Ni-N, Ni-C, and
the “obtuse triangle” path of Ni-(C/N) in the NiPc, with the rank ratio of 100%, 66.89%, 58.98%.
Asterisk “*” represents a fixed coordination number, CN is the coordination number, R is the
distance between absorber and backscatter atoms, σ2 is the Debye-Waller factor to account for both
thermal and structural disorders, ΔE0 is inner potential correction, and R factor indicates the
goodness of the fit.
S02 was first fixed to 0.93 according to the experimental EXAFS fit of NiPc by fixing CN as the
known crystallographic value, which was further verified in NiO and Ni foil. During the fitting
process for Ni-BTA, a fixed Ni-N coordination number of four was pre-set in the first coordination
sphere of Ni-BTA, meanwhile tailoring the axial-O structure (without or with a different number of
3
Ni-O paths). The ΔR for each path was allowed to vary independently, while ΔE0 and σ2 were allowed
to vary dependently. A reasonable range of EXAFS fitting parameters is: CN > 0; σ2 > 0 Å2; ΔE0 <
10 eV; R factor < 0.02 (2.0%), especially a smaller value indicates a better fitting result.
NiPc: 1.0 ≤ R (Å) ≤ 3.2, 3.0 ≤ k (Å-1) ≤ 12.0
NiO: 1.0 ≤ R (Å) ≤ 3.0, 3.0 ≤ k (Å-1) ≤ 12.0
Ni foil: 1.0 ≤ R (Å) ≤ 2.5, 3.0 ≤ k (Å-1) ≤ 12.0
Ni-BTA: 1.0 ≤ R (Å) ≤ 2.0, 2.0 ≤ k (Å-1) ≤ 12.0. …”
2. For the DFT-calculations, it is not clear if vibrational analysis was performed.
Response: For the DFT-calculations in our manuscript, the free energies are corrected for zero-point
vibrational energy (ZPE), enthalpy variation by temperature (∆H0→T), and entropy (S), as Eq. 6:
𝐺= 𝐸+ 𝑍𝑃𝐸+ ∆𝐻0→𝑇−𝑇𝑆 (6)
where E is the DFT-derived total ground state energy of a system, ZPE is the zero-point energy
correction, ∆H0→T represents the enthalpy variation by temperature, and TS is the entropy correction
computed with standard methods. These three parts are used to convert E into free energy (G) at
298.15 K (Appl. Catal. B: Environ. 2022, 316, 121591, J. Mater. Chem. A, 2021, 9, 26019-26027.).
The detailed values of the above parameters (EZPE, ∆H0→T and TS) are shown in Supplementary
Table 8 in the revised manuscript.
Meanwhile, we would like to express our sincere apologies to the reviewer for causing the
misunderstanding by the inaccurate representation of Eq. 6 on calculating the free energy (not
referring to the ∆H0→T) in our manuscript.

enthalpy variation by temperature; TS is the entropy contribution to the free energy at 298.15 K; and
∆ is thermodynamic modification value.

3. For the biased MD-calculations (Figure 5c, d), the parameters and computational methodology is
not given. Helmholtz free energy (F) is mentioned for the MD, but it is not clear how this quantity
was obtained from the simulations.
5
Response: To evaluate the kinetic barriers, we used a constrained AIMD method, namely, the “slow-
growth” approach to obtaining the free-energy profile at 300 K through different collective variables
(CV) (J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2021, 143,25, 9423-9428, Nat. Commun. 2021, 12,1, 4225). A cut-off energy
of 400 eV, a k-mesh grid of 1 × 1 × 1, a time step of 0.5 fs, and a canonical ensemble (NVT) were
adopted in the AIMD simulations. Slow growth is a method based on thermodynamic integration that
calculates the free energy profile along the reaction coordinate. In this method, the value of the
reaction coordinate (namely 𝜉̇) linearly changes from the characteristic value of the initial state (IS)
to that of the transition state (TS) with a velocity of transformation 𝜉̇. The free energy difference
between two states can be calculated by thermodynamic integration, as shown in Eq. 8:
𝑊𝐼𝑆→𝑇𝑆= ∫
𝜕𝐹
𝜕𝜉
𝜉(𝑇𝑆)
𝜉(𝐼𝑆)
∙𝜉̇𝑑𝑡 (8)
where F is the free energy calculated at the general coordinate 𝜉̇, which is evolving with t,
𝜕𝐹
𝜕𝜉 is
calculated along the track of a constrained MD through the SHAKE algorithm in VASP code, which
is specified in the output file REPORT. In the limit of infinitesimally small ∂𝜉, the work 𝑊𝐼𝑆→𝑇𝑆 is
the free-energy difference between the IS and TS. In this work, the bond lengths of the Ni-O and O-
O bonds were chosen as CV to describe the 2e– and 4e– processes of ORR, respectively, with 𝜉̇ value
of 0.0005 Å in each AIMD step.
In Figure 5c, d, the right vertical coordinate F (r, 300K) is the gradient of the free energy along
the limiting degrees of freedom (CV) at general coordinate 𝜉 (the bond lengths of Ni-O or O-O bonds
after the OOH species absorbed on the Ni-sites in d-OH-Ni model, also shorted as “r”) and 300K,
which is directly exported frame-by-frame from the AIMD calculation into REPORT file. The left
vertical coordinate free energy is obtained by integrating F.
To better illustrate this point, on Page 23 in the revised manuscript, we added: “…A cut off energy
of 400 eV, a k-mesh grid of 1 × 1 × 1, a time step of 0.5 fs, and a canonical ensemble (NVT) were
adopted in the AIMD simulations. …” and “… And the F (r, 300K) is the gradient of the free energy
along the limiting degrees of freedom (CV) at general coordinate 𝜉 (the bond lengths of Ni-O or O-O
bonds after the OOH species absorbed on the Ni-sites in d-OH-Ni model, shorted as “r”) and 300 K,
which is directly exported frame-by-frame from the AIMD calculation into REPORT file. The free
energy is obtained by integrating F…”

6. Supplementary Fig. 44, it is not clear how these error bars were obtained, this is not described.
Response: In the kinetic calculations, due to technical limitations, we are temporarily unable to
accurately account for the barriers induced by changes in water structure during the MD simulation.
Therefore, we minimized the error caused by the variation of the solution network on the potential
barrier by performing three slow-growth kinetic simulations (Figure R1).
To obtain the error bars, the three-group data were processed by Origin software to obtain the
corresponding average values and standard deviation. Data are presented as average values±standard
deviation from a representative experiment (n=3, independent samples), in which the standard
deviation acts as error bars. Meanwhile, due to the large number of data points, we chose to display
one error bar for every 100 data points in order to make the data plots concise, as shown in
Supplementary Fig. 44.
To better illustrate this point, the method of achieving the average values and error bars is added
following Supplementary Fig. 44 in the revised manuscript, as follows:
8
Figure R1. The Free energy changes as a function of CV along Ni-O bond O-O bond cleavage for
the 2e- and 4e--ORR pathways by performing three slow-growth kinetic simulations, respectively.
The initial state, transition state, and final state structures are denoted as IS, TS, and FS, respectively.
Supplementary Fig. 44. Kinetic energy barriers (with error bars) for 2e- and 4e--ORR from slow-
growth kinetic simulations. Data are presented as average values ± standard deviation from a
representative experiment (n=3, independent samples).
“...To minimize the error caused by the variation of the solution network on the potential barrier,
we performed three slow-growth kinetic simulations. And then, the three-group data were processed
by Origin software to obtain the corresponding average values and standard deviation, in which the
standard deviation as error bars. Meanwhile, due to the large number of data points, we chose to
display one error bar for every 100 data points in order to make the data plots concise, as shown in
Supplementary Fig. 44...”

and the effort in helping us improve the quality of this work to better match the standard of Nature
Communications.

3. Fig. 5d - two O-O bond distances were updated without any notification from the authors?
Response: We would like to thank the reviewer for the careful review. According to the reviewer’s
previous suggestion, we have calculated the error bar on the kinetic barriers by carrying out three
additional and individual slow-growth kinetic simulations, which all start with the optimized structure
of d-OH-Ni (see following Supplementary Fig. 44 and Supplementary Fig. 42), besides the original
result (previous Fig. 5c, d). In our last response, we chose one slow-growth kinetic simulation
(Supplementary Fig. 44a) from the three re-calculated results on the 4e−-ORR process to update the
previous data in Fig. 5c, which possesses a larger O-O bond elongation of over 1.0 Å.
Correspondingly, the O-O bond distances in Fig. 5d are updated based on the revised Fig. 5c.
Meanwhile, we are sorry that the corresponding atomic structures at different reaction states (IS, TS,
and FS) in Fig. 5d were not updated, which brings confusion to the reviewer.
4
The reasons for updating Fig. 5c are as follows:
First, the data in the previous and revised Fig. 5c deliver the same conclusion on confirming
the more favorable kinetics for 2e−-ORR than 4e−-ORR. That is, the 2e−-ORR has lower energy
barriers (0.33 eV shown in previous and revised Fig. 5c) than 4e−-ORR (1.17 eV shown in previous
Fig. 5c, d and 1.21 eV shown in the revised Fig. 5c).
Second, during the process of conducting the additional slow-growth kinetic simulations, the TS
of O-O bond cleavage commonly appears when the bond elongation is over 0.8 Å, or even close to
0.9 Å. For example, in the revised Fig. 5c (or Supplementary Fig. 43a), the free energy rapidly
increases to 1.18 eV at the O-O bond elongation of 0.68 Å, and then reaches the highest free energy
of 1.21 eV at the O-O bond elongation of 0.88 Å (2.35-1.47=0.88 Å). However, in our previous Fig.
5c, the calculation was set to stop at the O-O bond elongation of 0.9 Å, and FS was chosen at the O-
O bond elongation of 0.85 Å (2.32-1.47=0.85 Å). In our opinion, it seems that the previous calculation
was stopped at a too-early state. Thus, a longer slow-growth kinetic simulation with O-O bond
elongation over 1.0 Å is expected to deliver a more reasonable and rigorous result.
Thus, we chose one of the three re-calculated slow-growth kinetic simulations to replace the
previous data in Fig. 5c, d. We are very sorry for not clearly pointing out the changes in Fig. 5c, d in
our last response letter, which may confuse the reviewer.
The previous Fig.5 c, d.
The last revised Fig.5 c, d.

and FS) of O-O bond cleavage are updated in Fig. 5d as follows, and the corresponding energy barrier
is summarized in Supplementary Fig. 43. Meanwhile, the other two slow-growth kinetic simulations
with the updated one slow-growth kinetic simulation (as in Fig. 5c) are also provided in the revised
supporting information as Supplementary Fig. 44, with the corresponding atomic structures at
different reaction states (IS, TS, and FS).

Correspondingly, the description on Page 17 has also been revised as follows: “…For the 2e−-
ORR pathway, the rate-determining step was found to be the Ni-O elongation by the slow-growth
approach, with a reaction barrier of 0.33 eV (Fig. 5c, d, and Supplementary Fig. 43). In contrast,
the rate-determining step for 4e−-ORR changed to the formation of *O with a significantly higher
reaction barrier of 1.21 eV. In addition, to reduce the effect of solution network variation on the
barrier, we calculated the error bar on the kinetic barriers by three individual slow-growth kinetic
simulations (Supplementary Figs. 44, 45), and the similar results obtained from these simulations
(i.e., much higher energy required to break the O-O bond than that of the Ni-O bond) confirm that d-
OH-Ni would follow the energetically more favorable 2e−-ORR pathway, resulting in the rapid and
selective formation of H2O2…”
4. Fig. 5e - y-axis on the right hand side is also representing F, in the unit (eV/Angstrom), is this
correct?
Response: The reviewer means Fig. 5c? In Fig. 5c, the y-axis on the right-hand side is F (r, 300K),
not representing the typical F. The unit of F (r, 300K) is “eV/Angstrom”. As mentioned in the previous
response, F is the free energy, and its unit is (eV). While F (r, 300K) is the gradient of the free energy
(F) along the limiting degrees of freedom at general coordinate 𝜉 and 300 K, which possess the same
meaning with the
𝜕𝐹
𝜕𝜉 in our study. Additionally, 𝜉 means the bond length (r) or bond elongation (Δr)
of Ni-O or O-O bond after the OOH species absorbed on the Ni-sites in our d-OH-Ni model, thus the
unit of F (r, 300K) is (eV/Å).
We are sorry that the two terms both start with the same part of “F” for the free energy (F) and

5. 𝐺= 𝐸+ 𝑍𝑃𝐸+ ∆𝐻0→𝑇−𝑇𝑆- expression is incorrect.
Response: When defining the parameters or abbreviations in the expression of “𝐺= 𝐸+ 𝑍𝑃𝐸+
∆𝐻0→𝑇−𝑇𝑆”, we are sorry that the ZPE is wrongly written as EZPE in the last response letter. By
unifying ZPE and defining ZPE as the zero-point vibration energy correction, and after a careful
check, we can now confirm that the expression of “𝐺= 𝐸+ 𝑍𝑃𝐸+ ∆𝐻0→𝑇−𝑇𝑆” is correct. The
same expression could be found in various literature (Nat. Commun. 2023, 14, 1890, Nat. Commun.
2018, 9, 5422, and J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2017, 139, 5494, etc.), and the screenshots in these works on
the relevant calculations are also shown in the following form.
𝐺= 𝐸+ 𝑍𝑃𝐸+ ∆𝐻0→𝑇−𝑇𝑆 (6)
In our study, the expression (Eq. 6) is adopted to calculate G, rather than 𝛥𝐺 , and 𝛥𝐺 is
calculated according to Eq. 7, as follows.
𝛥𝐺( 𝑂
∗𝑂𝐻)= 𝐺( 𝑂
∗𝑂𝐻) +
3𝐺(𝐻2)
2
−𝐺(∗) −2𝐺(𝐻2𝑂) −𝑒𝑈 (7)
For Eq. 6, the reviewer may be confused by the extra part of “∆H0→T”, which represents the
temperature effect on enthalpy. As mentioned in previous works, when considering the temperature
effect of enthalpy, a correction of ∆H0→T should be added in the original equation of “𝐺= 𝐸+ 𝑍𝑃𝐸−
𝑇𝑆”. The “Δ” before “H” in Eq. 6 represents the temperature effect from 0 to 298.15 K on the enthalpy
correction. To calculate the Gibbs free energy (𝛥𝐺) for all the ORR steps in one equation, the 𝛥𝐺
could be defined as: 𝛥𝐺= 𝛥𝐸+ 𝛥𝑍𝑃𝐸+ ∆0→𝑇𝛥𝐻−𝑇𝛥𝑆. Especially, there are two “𝛥” before the
9
“H”, as mentioned in the following literature.
To sum up, we confirm the expression (𝐺= 𝐸+ 𝑍𝑃𝐸+ ∆𝐻0→𝑇−𝑇𝑆) is correct upon defining
ZPE as the zero-point vibration energy correction, and the related references have also been cited in
the revised manuscript, as follows. Meanwhile, we again express our apologies to the reviewer for
any confusion in previous manuscripts.

6. Furthermore, when talking about Suppl. Fig. 4, the reviewer meant Suppl. Table 4; which was not
spotted by the authors. Can the current densities can be added to Supp. Table 4?
Response: We would like to thank the reviewer for the suggestion. In Supplementary Table 4, we
provide a comparison of the H2O2 selectivity for Ni-BTA with the reported electrocatalysts obtained
by the RRDE (rotating ring disk electrode) test, rather than by the flow-cell test (as the
Supplementary Table 5 mentioned in Question 2). In the RRDE test, the selectivity and the potential
range of ORR for H2O2 production could be fairly compared. However, it is unfair to compare the
current densities directly in the disk electrode (ID) and ring electrode (Ir) in different works. This is
because ID is composed of the current densities of 4e–-ORR and 2–-ORR pathways and IR is closely
related to the parameters of RRDE. The RRDEs used in different reported works commonly have
different electrode areas for the ring and different collection efficiencies, which make the direct
comparison of the current densities unreasonable.
According to previous reports, the current density for H2O2 production (JH2O2) in the RRDE test
can be calculated based on the collection efficiency, ring current, and the area of the disk electrode,
according to the following equation:
JH2O2=Jring/N/Sdisk
11
where Jring is the current of the ring electrode, N is the collection efficiency of the RRDE, and
Sdisk is the area of the disk electrode (J. Am. Chem. Soc., 2024, 146, 9434-9443, J. Am. Chem. Soc.,
2021, 143, 7819-7827).
We believe that JH2O2 could be a relatively fair parameter for comparing the H2O2 yield in the
RRDE test for different catalysts. Thus, JH2O2, together with the current density of the disk electrode
(Jdisk) at 0.2 V vs. RHE, have been added in the revised Supplementary Table 4, as follows:

1) For Figure 1d, Ni-BTA with various Ni-(NH)4 coordination structures are discussed. It would be
better to provide the corresponding structures either within this figure or in the Supporting
Information to clarify each configuration.
Response: Firstly, we would like to thank the reviewer for his/her positive comments on the last
version of our manuscript and the very valuable suggestions that will definitely help us further
improve the quality of this work.
The Ni-(NH)4 coordination structures for Ni-BTA in our work include four configurations: s-Ni,
s-OH-Ni, d-Ni and d-OH-Ni, which represent the single-layer Ni-(NH)4 coordination, single-layer
Ni-(NH)4 with axial-OH, dual-layer Ni-(NH)4 coordination, and dual-layer Ni-(NH)4 with axial-OH,
respectively. The adsorption capability of these four Ni-(NH)4 coordination structures towards *OOH
is discussed in Fig. 1b, and the corresponding structures for the four coordination structures are
provided in Fig. 1a in our manuscript. To better illustrate the structure of these configurations, we
have unified the denotations of all these configurations in the revised Fig. 1.
For Fig. 1d, we have discussed the d-Ni and d-OH-Ni configurations, and their configurations
after adsorbing *OOH on the Ni-center in the bottom Ni-BTA layer, which is illustrated in Fig. 1e.
To make this illustration more straightforward, we have added new labels of the denotations of each
configuration in the revised Fig. 1e to more clearly show all the configurations discussed in Fig. 1d,
as follows.

2) Since selectivity is highlighted, providing free energy values for O* would help to compare the

and ΔGOOH* values in Supplementary Table 8 for clarification.

help compare the selectivity of the two reaction routes (2e− and 4e−-ORR) for the catalytic sites, and
a larger ΔG*O value than that of ∆GH2O2 would contribute to a higher 2e−-ORR selectivity, from a
thermodynamic aspect. In our study, the Ni sites on Ni-BTA deliver a smaller ΔG*O value than that
of ∆GH2O2, the difference is −0.26 eV as shown in Fig. 5b and Supplementary Fig. 41, and this is
the reason that we carried out the slow-growth kinetic simulations to better explain the superior 2e−-
ORR catalytic performance of Ni-BTA.

3) The equilibrium potential for the four-electron pathway (1.23 V vs. SHE) corresponds to 4.92 eV
(4 × 1.23), while the two-electron pathway (0.70 V vs. SHE) corresponds to 3.52 eV (4 × 1.23 − 2 ×
0.70). Authors are asked to make corrections in energy level diagrams and ensure the consistency of
these values throughout the manuscript.
Response: We would like to thank the reviewer for this constructive comment. According to the
theoretical calculations, the equilibrium potential for the two-electron ORR is 0.695 V vs. RHE, and
when rounding to two decimal places, it is usually set as 0.70 V. In our manuscript, the equilibrium
potentials for 4e− and 2e− pathway are set to 1.23 V and 0.695 V vs. RHE, and these values remained
consistent throughout the manuscript.
On this basis, as shown in Supplementary Fig. 41, the ∆𝐺∗+𝑂2 exhibits the value of 4.92 eV,
meanwhile ∆𝐺∗+𝐻2𝑂2 possesses a free energy of 3.53 eV (4 × 1.23 − 2 × 0.695), as indicated by the
reviewer.
To demonstrate the thermodynamic barrier for 2e−-ORR of the catalytic sites, we presented the
energy level diagrams at 0.695 V vs. RHE (i.e., the equilibrium potential of H2O2) in Fig. 5b, in which
the largest thermodynamic barrier for all steps from O2 to H2O2 represents the theoretical
overpotential for the material to catalyze 2e−-ORR (Nat. Catal. 2023, 6, 1164-1173, Angew. Chem.
Int. Ed. 2024, 63, e202405334, etc.). Meanwhile, a complete 4e−-ORR energy level diagram, not
stopping at H2O2, is also presented to directly compare the values of ΔG*O and ∆GH2O2 and study the
2e−-ORR selectivity from the thermodynamics aspect, as mentioned in the response to Comment 2.
According to the suggestion from the reviewer and to deliver better consistency in the energy
level diagram, we have re-plotted the energy level diagram at 1.23 V vs. RHE, in which the larger
thermodynamic barriers, even higher than 1.0 eV, almost in all oxygen reduction steps again indicate
their low activity for catalyzing 4e−-ORR. And to better explain the high 2e−-ORR selectivity for the
Ni-sites, the energy level diagram at 1.23 V vs. RHE is added in Supplementary Fig. 41b as follows.
Note: the free energies (i.e., the thermodynamic barrier) at U=0.695 V or 1.23 V vs. RHE for all
adsorption species are calculated as follows:

4) In Figure 5b, the d-OH-Ni configuration shows identical thermodynamic energy values for O₂*
and O*, while ΔGOOH* is nearly zero. This suggests errors in free energy calculations, which should
be re-checked. The authors are asked to avoid repeated mistakes and misrepresentations of the data.
Response: We would like to thank the reviewer for the careful review. Figure 5b is the free energy
(i.e., the thermodynamic barrier) profile of 2e− and 4e−-ORR pathways at the equilibrium potentials
of 0.695 V vs. RHE on different Ni-(NH)4 configurations. Please note that this is NOT calculated at
U=0 V vs. RHE. Under the equilibrium potential for 2e−-ORR, if it is an ideal electrochemical reaction,
the free energy of adsorbing all intermediates should be equal to 0 eV, corresponding to an ideal
overpotential of 0 V. The close, but not identical, values for ΔG*O₂ (−0.281 eV) and ΔG*O (−0.257 eV)
indicate the similar thermodynamic barrier between the adsorption of O₂ and the transformation from
*OOH to *O at an applied potential of U=0.695 V vs. RHE. Meanwhile, the ΔG*OOH is nearly zero
(−0.016 eV), indicating there is a thermodynamic barrier close to zero at U=0.695 V vs. RHE for the
adsorption of *OOH. Similar phenomena could be found in previous literature as well (Nat.
Nanotechol., 2024, https://doi.org/10.1038/s41565-024-01807-x, Nat. Commun., 2024, 15: 2062,
etc.), and the screenshots in these works on the relevant calculations are provided and discussed as
follows.
In addition, the adsorption energy values for the *O₂, *O, and *OOH are plotted in
Supplementary Fig. 41a (U=0 V vs. RHE), in which there are decreasing values for the
thermodynamic energy when adsorbing the *O₂, *O, and *OOH intermediates, which could make an
explanation of the reviewer’s concern on the close values of ΔG*O₂ (−0.281 eV) and ΔG*O (−0.257
eV) in Fig. 5b.
7
Taking the above into account, we can confirm that the calculations on the free energy are correct.
Fig. 5. The insights on oxygen reduction reaction for Ni-BTA. …b Free energy (thermodynamic
barrier) profile of 2e− and 4e−-ORR pathways at the equilibrium potentials of 0.695 V vs. RHE on
different Ni-(NH)4 configurations.
Supplementary Fig. 41. Free energy profile of 2e− and 4e−-ORR pathways at the potentials of U=0
V vs. RHE and U=1.23 V vs. RHE on different Ni-(NH)4 configurations.
Fig.5 b in Nature Communications, 2024,15:2062, in which the close values between the free energy
of *OOH, *OH and *O for specific catalysts at 1.23 V vs. RHE (the equilibrium potential for 4e−-
ORR) indicate their close thermodynamic barriers. For example, the close values and near to 0 eV for
8
the ΔGO* and ΔGOH* of Fe SAs-MCP indicate a nearly ideal adsorption states to *OH and *O for Fe
SAs-MCP when occurring 4e−-ORR.
Fig. 4 g in Nature Nanotechnology, 2024, https://doi.org/10.1038/s41565-024-01807-x, for OER
process, at 1.23 V vs. RHE, the Co-site delivers all free energies near to 0 eV for *OH, *O and *OOH,
indicating its small overpotential for oxygen evolution.

As for the second comment, Supplementary Fig. 43 presents the calculated kinetic barriers for
*OOH to H2O2 or *OOH to *O on Ni-sites of d-OH-Ni for the 2e− and 4e−-ORR pathways,
respectively. It is a conclusion of the slow-growth kinetic simulation (Fig. 5c, d). The final states and
their free energies are achieved from the stable state of H2O2 (for the 2e− pathway) and *O+H2O (for
the 4e− pathway) in Supplementary Fig. 41.
In our study, for the 2e−-ORR pathway, the rate-determining step was set to the cleavage of the
Ni-O bond between the adsorbed *OOH and the Ni-sites, which delivers a reaction barrier of 0.33 eV
according to the Ni-O elongation in the slow-growth simulation, with the formation of desorbed OOH
species. In contrast, the rate-determining step for 4e−-ORR was set to the cleavage of the O-O bond
in the adsorbed *OOH with the formation of *O and desorbed OH species, with a significantly higher
reaction barrier of 1.21 eV. Because we only focus on explaining the high 2e−-ORR selectivity from
a dynamic point of view, the larger reaction barrier for breaking the O-O bond than the Ni-O bond in
the Ni-*OOH configuration should be sufficiently reasonable to explain the high 2e−-ORR selectivity
for the Ni-BTA catalyst. Thus, we did not further simulate the subsequent protonation after the
desorption of the *OOH species. Consequently, the transition state geometries of this process were
not included in Supplementary Fig. 43. Nevertheless, we totally agree that the transformation from
10
*OOH into H₂O₂ is also practically important for the formation of H2O2, and we would make a deeper
study on this point in our future works.
In addition, when responding to the question, we also find that a volcano-like curve is more
appropriate to describe the transition from IS toTS. Thus, we have re-plotted Supplementary Fig. 43
as follows:
Supplementary Fig. 43. The calculated kinetic barriers for *OOH to H2O2 or *O on Ni-sites in the
model of d-OH-Ni for 2e− and 4e−-ORR pathways, respectively.
Lastly, we would like to express our sincere gratitude again for your time reviewing this article
and the effort in helping us to improve the quality of this work to better match the standard of Nature
Communications.

RESPONSE TO REVIEWER COMMENTS
Reviewer #3 (Remarks to the Author):
I have no further comments, however, I request that the authors include the atomic coordinates of all
reaction intermediates to ensure that the computational work can be reproduced by the academic
community.
Response: Firstly, we would like to thank the reviewer for his/her positive comments on the last
version of our manuscript.
The atomic coordinates of all Ni-BTA based models, and the molecular dynamics trajectories,
including the initial and final configurations, are provided in Supplementary Data 1.

and the effort in helping us to improve the quality of this work to better match the standard of Nature
